4:Ta4e,方法上，论文把问题形式化为两源混合训练。目标域有固定唯一 token 数 D_target，总训练预算是 D_total，目标域在训练中的占比为 h。于是目标域平均重复次数 r 由 h、D_total、D_target 共同决定；通用域被视为足够大，不发生重复。核心建模对象是目标域 loss 如何依赖模型规模 N、目标数据规模 D_target、以及目标域训练 token 数 h·D_total，或者等价地依赖重复次数 r。

经验层面，作者做了大规模 sweep：超过 2000 次训练，模型规模从 101M 到 805M，覆盖多种目标数据大小和多种数据类型。数据设置分三类。多语言：以 FineWeb2 中德语为主，人工截断到 50M、100M、500M、1B token，并设无限数据对照；通用域是英语 FineWeb。还在法语和斯瓦希里语上做验证。多领域：目标域包括 OpenWebMath、Wikipedia、peS2o 科学论文等，通用域来自 DCLM、FineWeb、Nemotron-CC 等。质量过滤：对 DCLM 用 fastText 质量分类器打分，取 top 1/5/10/20% 作为“高质量但受限”的目标域，再与其余更充足数据混合。这样设计的好处是，它不是只在“英语+某专业语料”上得出结论，而是测试规律是否跨语言、跨领域、跨质量筛选机制稳定。

论文的关键观察有几个。其一，重复带来的收益是平滑递减的，而不是到某个阈值突然失效；过拟合也不是简单由重复次数单独决定，而是由“重复次数 × 模型规模 × 训练预算”共同塑造。其二，在 mixture setting 下，通用域持续提供新 token，会延缓单源重复训练中的退化，因此目标域可以承受远高于单源训练经验上限的重复。其三，大模型一方面更容易在目标域验证集上出现过拟合拐点，另一方面也更能从有限目标数据中榨出收益；也就是“学得更快，也更容易过头”。

在此基础上，作者拟合了一个目标域 loss 的 scaling law。虽然全文给出的具体函数形式带有若干可学习系数，但思想很清楚：把目标域 loss 分解为若干随模型规模、目标唯一数据量、目标域训练暴露量变化的幂律项，并显式建模重复导致的收益递减。这个形式既能覆盖“更多唯一数据更好”，也能覆盖“同一批数据重复仍有价值，但边际收益下降”。作者用小模型、小预算子集拟合参数，再外推到更大模型和更大预算，检验是否能预测最优 h 或最优 r。论文还把框架扩展到两个都受限的目标域，讨论多受限源之间如何分配预算。5:T415,This is important because it turns a recurring recipe-design problem into something measurable and, to some extent, predictable. Many pretraining scenarios fit this setup: low-resource language adaptation, domain injection for math/science/code, and mixing a small high-quality subset with a large generic corpus. The main empirical correction is that the familiar single-source repetition ceiling does not transfer to mixture training. When fresh generic tokens continue to arrive, the scarce target source can often be repeated much more aggressively, with optimal target-task repetition reaching roughly 15–20x in several settings. That changes how one should think about curriculum design, mixture weights, and data budgeting. The second contribution is a fitted scaling law that can be estimated at small scale and extrapolated to larger models and budgets, reducing the need for expensive large-scale sweeps. The paper does not introduce a new architecture; its value is in making a high-frequency pretraining decision more quantitative.6:T922,The setup is a two-source mixture. The target source has a fixed number of unique tokens D_target, the total training budget is D_total, and h is the fraction of training tokens drawn from the target source. This induces a repetition factor r for the target source, while the generic source is assumed large enough to avoid repetition. The modeling goal is to predict target-domain loss as a function of model size, target-data size, and target exposure under a fixed compute budget.

The empirical study is broad: more than 2,000 training runs, model sizes from 101M to 805M parameters, and several target-data regimes. The paper covers three families of constrained data. For multilingual experiments, German from FineWeb2 is artificially capped at 50M, 100M, 500M, and 1B tokens, with English FineWeb as the abundant generic source; French and Swahili are used to test whether the pattern is language-specific. For domain mixtures, the target source includes OpenWebMath, Wikipedia, and peS2o, mixed with larger generic corpora such as DCLM, FineWeb, and Nemotron-CC. For quality-filtered mixtures, the authors score DCLM documents with a FastText quality classifier and treat the top 1/5/10/20% subsets as scarce high-quality targets.

The main empirical pattern is that repetition has smooth diminishing returns rather than a hard threshold, and the useful repetition range depends jointly on model size, target-data size, and total budget. Larger models overfit the target validation set sooner, but they also extract more value from limited target data before that point. The presence of fresh generic data changes the regime relative to single-source repetition: it keeps the model learning from new tokens even while the target source is being revisited many times.

On top of these observations, the paper fits a scaling law for target-domain loss. The exact parameterization is learned from data, but the conceptual structure is clear: it separates the effect of unique target data from the effect of repeated target exposure, and models both with smooth power-law-like terms. The law is then used to predict the optimal mixture weight or repetition count. The framework is also extended to the case of two scarce target domains, where the problem becomes one of allocating limited exposure across multiple constrained sources.7:T779,The experimental section is the core of the paper. It reports 2,000+ runs with models ranging from 101M to 805M parameters. In the multilingual setting, German target data spans 50M, 100M, 500M, and 1B tokens plus an unconstrained reference; French and Swahili are used as additional checks. In the domain setting, OpenWebMath is capped at 10M, 100M, and 1B tokens, and Wikipedia and peS2o are also studied under constrained budgets. In the quality-filtering setting, top-ranked DCLM subsets are treated as scarce high-quality targets.

Three results matter most. First, the optimal repetition count in mixture pretraining is often far above the familiar single-source heuristic. For target-domain performance, the paper repeatedly finds useful optima around 15–20 repetitions in several settings. Second, as the total compute budget grows, the optimal target fraction h tends to decrease, while the optimal repetition count r can still increase, because the same fixed target pool is revisited more times over a longer run. This is a subtle but operationally important point: a fixed mixture ratio is not compute-invariant. Third, larger models can benefit from higher repetition on scarce target data, but their optimum is sharper because overfitting arrives sooner on the target validation set.

The scaling-law validation is another major result. Parameters fitted on smaller-scale runs are shown to extrapolate reasonably well to larger scales, predicting target-domain loss curves and the optimal repetition level without re-running a full large-scale sweep. The paper also reports that the qualitative pattern holds across multilingual, domain-specific, and quality-filtered mixtures, which suggests the phenomenon is not tied to one corpus family. The two-scarce-domain extension further shows that the framework can provide sensible allocation trends when more than one valuable source is data-limited.8:T6b5,Start with the introduction and the first two figures. They establish the actual question: not generic data mixing, but optimal exposure of a scarce target source under a fixed compute budget. Then read the setup carefully, especially the relationship among D_target, D_total, h, and r. A common failure mode is to think only in terms of mixture ratio; the paper is stronger when read through the lens of induced repetition.

For a close read, focus on four parts. First, the main result plots showing how the optimum moves with model size and budget. This is where the paper makes clear why a fixed target ratio is not a robust recipe. Second, the scaling-law form and fitting procedure. The key is not memorizing the equation, but understanding how the law separates unique-data effects from repeated-exposure effects. Third, compare the multilingual, domain, and quality-filtered sections to see what is stable and what is dataset-specific. Fourth, read the extension to two constrained target domains; that section is closer to real recipe design than the simplest one-target-plus-generic setup.

If time is limited, read the introduction, the experimental setup, Figures 1 and 2, the scaling-law extrapolation results, and the multi-target extension. That is enough to capture the main contribution in under an hour. If the goal is to operationalize the result, spend more time on the appendix details for fitting, parameter stability across data families, and the exact target-domain evaluation protocol. Two follow-up questions are especially worth thinking about: whether the law still holds at much larger model scales, and how it changes when the generic source is not truly fresh and also begins to repeat.9:T42d,The analysis starts from optimal finite-context prediction and treats representation changes as changes to the conditional distribution available inside a fixed window. For fragmentation, each source symbol is recoded into several smaller units without losing information. The paper shows that losslessness does not imply finite-window equivalence: after recoding, a predictor operates on sub-symbol positions with boundary, phase, and partial-history effects, and the best achievable loss can be strictly worse than the source-level finite-context optimum. This is an intrinsic representation effect, not just an optimization failure. For greedy tokenization, the authors study dictionary-style parsing into longer emitted units. A token window covers a random number of source symbols. If that span is large enough, a token-level predictor can match a longer source-context predictor without asymptotic loss. In the typical-window case, the achievability guarantee is controlled by the lower tail of the source-span distribution and by the tokenizer compression rate.a:T427,They run a controlled study with the same Transformer backbone and the same corpus (Slimpajama-627B), varying only (i) representation: bytes (UTF-8, V=256) vs Llama2 BPE (V=32k), and (ii) objective: AR vs continuous-time discrete masked diffusion. Comparisons are compute-matched by normalizing the amount of raw information per step: byte models use longer contexts (L_byte=8192) while BPE uses shorter contexts (L_BPE=1792), and scaling is plotted against total FLOPs.
Mechanistically, they argue AR byte models can recover efficiency via emergent segmentation: predictive entropy aligns with BPE boundaries, so once early bytes of a wordpiece are known, the remaining intra-piece bytes become easy. MDM breaks this advantage because stochastic masking frequently removes the very sequential cues needed to collapse intra-word uncertainty. They further isolate the role of context destruction with controlled permutation experiments that separately disrupt local context vs sequential integrity, showing byte diffusion is uniquely sensitive to preserving order.b:T433,It first isolates two potential causes of slow MDM training: the locality bias of natural language (predictive information for a token is concentrated in nearby positions) and the complexity of the any-order generation problem space. Controlled experiments confirm locality bias is the dominant factor: samples with very high context (almost no masking) or very low context (almost full masking) provide almost no effective learning signal, wasting most of the training budget. Based on this analysis, it proposes bell-shaped time sampling, which adjusts the diffusion timestep sampling distribution to increase the probability of sampling timesteps corresponding to intermediate context levels and reduce weight on high/low context timesteps. Unlike most existing MDM optimizations that focus on inference speed or loss function modification, this method requires no changes to model architecture or training objective, only adjusts training-time timestep sampling, and is compatible with the common large-scale MDM setup of continual pretraining from an ARM initialization.c:T53a,The setup has three stages. A model is pretrained on a broad corpus D_pre, post-trained on a smaller target corpus D_post, and then fine-tuned by a downstream user on a new objective D_ft. The main intervention is to mix part of D_post into the pretraining stage before the dedicated post-training stage. The evaluation is not based on immediate post-training loss alone. Instead, the downstream fine-tuning method is fixed, the learning rate is swept, and the resulting frontier between downstream loss, retained D_post capability, and general D_pre performance is compared across upstream recipes. This is the right measurement because two models with similar post-training loss can occupy different retention frontiers after fine-tuning. The paper also runs compute-matched allocation experiments, comparing all early exposure, all late exposure, and mixed schedules. The optimum tends to lie between the extremes. The proposed explanation is feature-level: early exposure lets the target capability be represented in more specialized and less interfering features, making it less vulnerable to later updates. Replay and dropout are evaluated under the same lens. Replay reduces overwriting by interleaving earlier data, while dropout discourages feature co-adaptation; both provide gains that are complementary to early data exposure.d:T474,The Marginal-Conditioned Bridge (MCB) sampler is proposed: at each reverse sampling step, first compute the token posterior marginal for each position output by the FLM based on the current continuous state, independently sample a valid one-hot token at each position as the endpoint for this step, then sample the continuous state for the next noise level from the analytical Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) bridge distribution conditioned on both the current state and the sampled complete one-hot endpoint. This method essentially replaces the original bridge to a single conditional mean with a mixture distribution of bridges to all valid one-hot sequences. Complete theoretical analysis is provided: the error from factorizing the joint posterior into the product of token marginals equals exactly the conditional multi-information among token positions; the one-step transition kernel of MCB preserves all token-wise posterior-predictive marginals, only losing residual cross-position dependence; under exact marginals and standard regularity assumptions, the path-space KL error of MCB is no larger than that of the conditional-mean bridge.e:T638,The paper has three layers of analysis. First, it evaluates 20 off-the-shelf DLMs and ARMs under a common generation setup using three metrics: n-gram entropy as a proxy for token-level randomness, semantic coherence for local sentence-level consistency, and semantic diversity for document-level variation. The empirical pattern is consistent: DLM outputs tend to have lower entropy, higher coherence, and higher diversity.

Second, it runs controlled objective studies. Instead of only comparing end-to-end systems, the authors align decoding and vary the training objective. They interpolate between ARM-like and DLM-like objectives and isolate components such as bidirectional context, input masking, label masking, and noise-level weighting. The main finding is that bidirectional context is the dominant driver of the coherence and diversity gains. Other ingredients of the DLM objective contribute much less.

Third, it studies decoding. The focus is block-wise iterative decoding with four remasking strategies: low-confidence, dynamic low-confidence, high-entropy, and random remasking. The key result is that entropy reduction is strongly tied to confidence-based remasking. Intuitively, these strategies repeatedly preserve high-confidence positions and revisit uncertain ones, which narrows the effective sample distribution. In contrast, random or high-entropy remasking leaves entropy roughly unchanged and close to the ARM level. The paper also provides a theoretical account of why remasking changes the resulting distribution rather than merely affecting decoding efficiency.f:T58c,There are three main experimental blocks. First, the large-scale comparison over 20 released models establishes the descriptive pattern: DLMs usually generate text with lower n-gram entropy but higher semantic coherence and semantic diversity than ARMs. The authors are careful to treat n-gram entropy as a proxy rather than a full entropy estimate.

Second, the controlled objective experiments are the most informative part. Under a shared decoding setup, the authors gradually move between DLM-style and ARM-style training formulations. The results show that keeping bidirectional context preserves most of the gains in coherence and diversity, while removing it substantially weakens them. By contrast, input masking details, label masking, and weighting functions have much smaller marginal effects.

Third, the decoding ablations compare low-confidence, dynamic low-confidence, high-entropy, and random remasking. Confidence-based remasking noticeably lowers entropy and pushes DLM outputs below ARM entropy. Other remasking strategies do not produce the same drop; entropy stays approximately at the ARM level. This is the paper's clearest causal claim: the low-entropy behavior often associated with DLMs is largely induced by the decoder.

The contribution is not a benchmark win but a careful behavioral decomposition with reasonably clean controls, which is more useful than another headline score comparison.10:T48f,OSDN adds a diagonal preconditioner D_t = diag(d_t) on top of the usual scalar gate in the Delta Rule. Since the Delta update corresponds to gradient descent on 1/2 ||S k_t - v_t||^2, the new method right-preconditions the gradient. The key algebraic result is that this right-preconditioning is exactly equivalent to scaling the write-side key feature by feature: \tilde{k}_t = d_t ⊙ k_t. As a result, the implementation can keep the existing chunkwise WY pipeline and only replace the storage-side K with \tilde{K}. The preconditioner d_t is updated online using OSGM-style hypergradient feedback. Its update depends only on keys and scalar gates, not on the high-dimensional memory state, values, or residuals, so the full d_t trajectory can be computed as an O(K)-state affine scan before the write pass. The APF variant adds learned token-wise and head-wise forgetting for the preconditioner only, which helps under non-stationary contexts without decaying the main memory state. The theory gives a population-limit super-geometric rate against a right-Newton comparator and a token-local contraction bound for the residual-ratio surrogate used in diagnostics.11:T9af,方法叫 Discrete Stochastic Localization（DSL）。核心设计是把离散 token 映射到单位球面上的 embedding，然后定义一个 stochastic localization 观测通道，而不是直接用常规 VP/VE Gaussian diffusion。作者先从一个全句共享全局 SNR 的高斯观测通道出发，写出 z_t 与干净 embedding x 的关系，并指出其等价的无条件 SDE 的 drift 是 MMSE denoiser，也就是 E[x | z_t]。到这里还只是连续扩散的常规视角。

关键变化发生在单位球几何上。若 clean token embeddings 都放在 unit sphere 上，并采用作者构造的 localization channel，则 Bayes-optimal clean-token posterior 只依赖 noisy state 本身，而不依赖名义上的 SNR/timestep。也就是说，同一个 noisy token state 已经包含了恢复 clean token 所需的全部信息，模型不需要额外的时间标签来解释“当前噪声有多大”。这就得到一个 time-invariant denoiser。

这个性质带来两个直接后果：
1. 每个 token 位置可以走自己的 SNR path，而不必全句共享同一个全局时间步。于是不同位置可以异步更新，有些位置像 masked refinement 一样从低信息到高信息逐步揭示，有些位置则可以按 random-order 的方式先确定再条件化后续位置。
2. masked diffusion 的端点行为成为 DSL 的特例，而连续有限 SNR 状态则提供了更细粒度的中间修正空间。于是 endpoint revealing、连续去噪、混合采样不再需要不同模型，而是同一个 posterior estimator 的不同查询方式。

为了让这个框架能在标准 Transformer/DiT 上训练，作者还引入了 mixed-support SNR sampling，让模型同时见到端点状态和有限 SNR 状态；并通过 mixture-of-tokens converter，把连续状态上的 posterior 学习转成可训练的离散 token 目标。论文还给出两个 exact NLL 视角来说明训练目标的状态支持是合理的，这一点是它比很多“只是换个噪声参数化”的工作更扎实的地方。

和已有 MDLM 的区别在于：MDLM 主要工作在离散 mask/no-mask 端点或有限离散状态上；DSL 保留了 mask-like 零信息端点，但在中间引入连续状态，同时不要求 denoiser 显式依赖时间。和传统 continuous diffusion LM 的区别在于：DSL 不是把 token embedding 扔进普通高斯扩散，而是专门设计了几何与通道，使 noisy state 自身就足以决定 clean posterior。12:T974,The method is called Discrete Stochastic Localization (DSL). The core idea is to place clean token embeddings on the unit sphere and define a stochastic-localization observation channel, instead of using a standard VP/VE Gaussian diffusion directly in embedding space. The paper starts from a sentence-level Gaussian observation channel with a global SNR and derives the equivalent unconditional SDE whose drift is the MMSE denoiser, namely the posterior mean E[x | z_t]. Up to that point, the setup still resembles ordinary continuous diffusion.

The key step is the unit-sphere geometry. Under the proposed localization channel, the Bayes-optimal clean-token posterior becomes a function of the noisy state alone and does not depend on the nominal SNR. In other words, the noisy token state already contains all the information needed for clean-token prediction, so the denoiser does not need an explicit timestep embedding to calibrate uncertainty. This yields a time-invariant denoiser.

That property has two major consequences.
1. Different token positions can follow different SNR paths. The model is no longer tied to a single global diffusion time for the whole sequence. Some positions can behave like masked refinement, while others can be revealed in a random-order autoregressive manner.
2. Endpoint masked diffusion becomes a special case of the same framework, while finite-SNR continuous states provide richer intermediate refinement. As a result, endpoint revealing, continuous denoising, and hybrid samplers can all be served by one posterior estimator rather than separate models.

For practical training, the paper introduces mixed-support SNR sampling so the model sees both endpoint states and finite-SNR states during training. A mixture-of-tokens converter maps the continuous-state posterior-learning problem into a trainable objective on a standard Transformer/DiT-style backbone. The paper also provides two exact NLL views to justify the support of the training objective, which makes the proposal more principled than a simple reparameterization trick.

Relative to MDLM, DSL keeps the mask-like zero-information endpoint but adds continuous intermediate states. Relative to standard continuous diffusion LMs, DSL is not just Gaussian noise over token embeddings; it is a deliberately designed geometry-plus-channel pair that makes the noisy state itself sufficient for posterior prediction.13:T67d,The experiments are mainly on OpenWebText, and the most important empirical claim is that a single checkpoint supports multiple decoding paths. The model is obtained by fine-tuning a pretrained MDLM checkpoint rather than training from scratch.

On masked-refinement decoding, using a ReMDM-family decoder, DSL improves distributional faithfulness measured by MAUVE across a range of step budgets from T=128 to T=1024. That result matters because it shows DSL does not give up the practical strengths of masked diffusion; instead, it uses continuous intermediate states to improve few-step generation.

The same checkpoint also supports random-order autoregressive sampling without retraining. This is more than a convenience result. It is direct evidence for the paper’s central claim that the model learns a clean-token posterior estimator rather than a sampler-specific reverse process.

The paper further presents a hybrid continuous-then-discrete sampler that can produce coherent generations in as few as T=48 steps. For non-autoregressive generation, this suggests a useful division of labor: continuous states for fast coarse refinement early on, followed by discrete revealing later.

The paper also reports competitive likelihood on Text8, although the emphasis appears to be more on generation quality and decoding flexibility than on pushing NLL alone. The provided excerpt does not include the full result tables, so I cannot reliably quote exact MAUVE values, parameter counts, or training-token totals. What is clear is that the evaluation covers masked refinement, random-order AR, and hybrid sampling, all from the same fine-tuned checkpoint.14:T5d4,A good skim follows four steps: problem framing, core theoretical claim, decoding implications, and empirical validation.

Start with the introduction. It gives a clear diagnosis of why continuous diffusion has lagged behind MDLM-style models for text: the issue is not continuity itself, but the mismatch between the corruption representation and the discrete nature of token uncertainty.

Then read Section 2.2 carefully. The unit-sphere embedding geometry and the resulting SNR-invariant Bayes-optimal posterior are the conceptual center of the paper. If that point is convincing, the rest of the framework becomes much easier to interpret.

Next, read Section 2.3 on per-token SNR paths. This is where the paper explains why masked refinement, random-order AR, and hybrid decoding can all be seen as different query patterns over the same posterior estimator. The important point is that the unification is model-level, not just a loose combination of decoders.

In the experiments, prioritize three things:
- MAUVE improvements on OpenWebText under few-step masked refinement;
- evidence that the same checkpoint supports random-order AR;
- the T=48 hybrid-sampling results.
A 30-minute skim is enough to decide whether the paper is worth deeper study: introduction, Sections 2.2 and 2.3, and the main experimental figures. If you want to judge whether DSL could scale into a serious pretraining paradigm, then also read the exact-NLL justification and the training-objective derivation.15:T583,The setup is deliberately controlled. The base stack is a decoder-only, LLaMA-style transformer trained on TinyStories. Everything is held fixed except the FFN module, which is replaced by a Mixtral-style MoE block in the sparse variants. The main sparse recipe uses 4 experts, top-2 routing, dropless dispatch, Switch-style load balancing, and optional router z-loss. The authors explicitly avoid token dropping and capacity truncation so that the comparison stays focused on routing behavior rather than execution shortcuts. The training objective combines next-token cross-entropy with a load-balancing auxiliary loss and an optional router z-loss; the best sparse recipe uses lambda_bal = 1e-2 and lambda_z = 1e-3. Fairness is evaluated in two ways. The active-match dense baseline is width-resized to approximately match the MoE’s per-token active parameter count. The total-match dense baseline is resized to match the MoE’s full stored parameter count. The headline configurations are: dense active-match at about 14.89M parameters, MoE at about 21.06M total and 14.77M active parameters, and dense total-match at about 21.06M parameters. Matching is tight, with less than 1% error for active counts and less than 0.1% for total counts. This makes the interpretation straightforward: if fairness means active routed capacity, MoE wins; if fairness means total capacity, dense still has a slight edge.16:T501,There are three main empirical takeaways. First, naive sparse training does not work automatically at tiny scale. Top-1 routing without balancing collapses almost immediately, with most layers sending nearly all tokens to one expert. Second, stable sparse training comes from a small recipe rather than a single trick: Switch-style balancing removes the immediate collapse, top-2 routing delivers the main quality gain over top-1, and router z-loss mostly improves routing behavior and stability diagnostics rather than being the main source of validation-loss improvement. Third, the headline numbers are clean. In the three-seed, full-data comparison, the dense active-match model reaches a best validation loss of 1.6545 ± 0.0012, the MoE reaches 1.5788 ± 0.0020, and the dense total-match model reaches 1.5608 ± 0.00x, slightly better than the MoE. So MoE clearly beats the active-matched dense baseline, but still trails the total-matched dense baseline by a small margin. The paper also shows that the sparse gain comes with healthy routing diagnostics rather than pathological expert collapse. The primary metrics are held-out validation loss and perplexity; throughput and single-GPU dispatch optimizations are treated as appendix-side support rather than core evidence.17:T41b,Its relevance to pretraining is through attention design and the train-inference interface, not generic serving optimization. If doubly-stochastic attention is indeed useful for reducing attention sinks and rank collapse, then inference cost is one of the main barriers to broader adoption. ASAP contributes a train-then-compile recipe: train with a standard finite-step Sinkhorn layer, then learn a lightweight parametric map that predicts the dual variables the Sinkhorn loop would otherwise solve for online. Two ideas matter here. First, some structured but expensive inference-time solvers may be replaceable by learned surrogates without changing the training recipe. Second, transport-based attention can be decomposed into a teacher operator plus a compiled dual predictor, rather than treated as a monolithic alternative to softmax. The contribution is best read as a practical step in attention-layer design; whether it matters for large causal LMs still depends on evidence at longer context, larger scale, and under autoregressive masking.18:T6b0,The method treats a trained Sinkhorn attention layer as a frozen teacher and learns to approximate its query-side dual variables. The pipeline is straightforward. First, train a Transformer with finite-step Sinkhorn normalization in the attention layer. Importantly, the target is not the infinite-step optimal transport solution, but the actual finite-budget operator used in practice. Second, freeze the teacher and run a small unlabeled calibration set through it. From each forward pass, extract the query-side normalization vector produced by the Sinkhorn loop, i.e. the source dual under the finite entropic transport convention. Third, on the same query and key activations, compute exact one-dimensional transport potentials along multiple sorted projection directions. These sliced geometric quantities become features for a lightweight regressor. Fourth, fit a parametric map from the sliced features to the teacher’s source dual, using either least-squares or convex-KL calibration. Fifth, at inference time, predict the source dual directly and recover the key-side dual analytically via an entropic c-transform, yielding a compiled approximation to the original doubly-stochastic operator. The paper also studies one-sided and two-sided c-transform variants. Relative to ESPFormer-style sliced transport, ASAP does not replace the training operator or rely on soft sorting during training; it is a post-training compilation step designed to mimic a trained Sinkhorn teacher. Relative to simply reducing the number of Sinkhorn iterations, it aims to approximate the teacher’s actual fixed-point behavior under the observed activation distribution, which is a more targeted operator approximation.19:T500,The most concrete numbers in the visible text concern training cost. Under a matched-resource IMDb setup, the SoftSort-based training operator used in the sliced-transport baseline requires 5.7 times more peak memory and is 14.8 times slower per training example than a Sinkhorn teacher with S = 10 iterations. This cost gap motivates the train-with-Sinkhorn, compile-afterward strategy. The paper evaluates ASAP on both language and vision benchmarks, comparing the original iterative Sinkhorn teacher, sliced-transport baselines, and the compiled ASAP variants. The stated goal is not to beat the teacher, but to obtain significant layer-level speedups while remaining close to the teacher’s performance and preserving the doubly-stochastic structure as much as possible. The provided text does not include the full result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent task-level accuracy numbers. When reading the experiments, the important checks are: how close the compiled layer stays to the teacher under different calibration losses; whether one-sided versus two-sided c-transform changes the speed-accuracy tradeoff; how approximation error scales with sequence length, depth, and modality; and whether ASAP is consistently better than simply using fewer Sinkhorn iterations.1a:T417,Start with the introduction, which frames the trilemma clearly: Sinkhorn is cheaper to train but slower at inference, while sliced transport is faster at inference but expensive to train. Then read the background on doubly-stochastic attention and finite-step Sinkhorn to understand what the dual variables represent. The method section is the main part to focus on, especially the calibration pipeline, the construction of sliced dual features, and the c-transform used to recover the second dual. In the experiments, prioritize the resource comparison table and the main benchmark table, then read the ablations on number of slices, calibration set size, regression objective, and one-sided versus two-sided compilation. Around 35 to 50 minutes should be enough for a solid skim. For pretraining-oriented readers, two follow-up questions are worth keeping in mind: whether the compilation idea survives causal masking, and whether it composes well with long-context attention schemes rather than only moderate-length language or vision settings.1b:T4b3,The study uses Qwen2.5-VL-7B as the base model and extends its context window from 32K to 128K. Following a Dynamic-NTK heuristic, the mRoPE base frequency is increased from 1×10^6 to 4×10^6. Each LongPT run uses a fixed 5B-token budget, a maximum sequence length of 131,072 tokens, and a global batch size of 4M tokens. On the data side, the authors build a pool of more than 1.5 million PDF documents spanning papers, books, technical manuals, and domains such as engineering, medicine, social sciences, and biology. Documents with 32 to 50 pages are selected and rendered into page images. With the 2×2 pixel unshuffle in the Qwen2.5-VL backbone, these documents produce multimodal sequences from roughly 32K to 128K tokens. Potential evaluation contamination is filtered using SHA-256 hashes of PDF content. Five long-document training tasks are synthesized and grouped into long-document VQA and OCR transcription. The final recipe emphasizes VQA-style supervision because it provides instruction formatting, information extraction, cross-page retrieval, numerical reasoning, and layout understanding signals. The resulting model is named MMProLong, and the recipe is also validated on Qwen3-VL.1c:T45d,The main training setup uses a 7B base model, 5B tokens of continued pretraining, 128K maximum training length, and a 4M-token global batch. MMProLong improves long-document VQA performance by 7.1% at 64K and 128K. Without additional training or adaptation, it maintains strong performance at 256K and 512K and exceeds baselines by more than 20%. The gains transfer to broader multimodal long-context tasks, including webpage-based needle-in-a-haystack on MM-NIAH, long-context compression on VTCBench, and long-video understanding benchmarks. The ablations are the most informative part. Balanced length distributions outperform target-length-focused data, indicating that the model should learn retrieval across many lengths and positions instead of specializing to the training-window boundary. Retrieval-heavy task mixtures are preferred because key-information retrieval remains the dominant weakness. A small amount of reasoning data helps preserve task diversity. Pure long-document VQA has limited negative impact on short-context capability, likely because the data remains instruction-formatted and diverse.1d:T4cb,The main contribution is a benchmark and metric suite. On the data side, the authors start from more than 100 million raw PDFs, down-select roughly 250k candidates through stratified sampling, then apply a two-stage MLLM-based annotation process for domain and subcategory classification. The final benchmark contains 711 documents spanning 7 domains, 30 subcategories, and 2 languages, with 1,897 questions and an average document length of 40.6 pages.

For annotation, they build an automated pipeline combining document parsers and MLLMs to generate questions, answers, and fine-grained evidence regions. The goal is scalability and consistency, since manual box-level annotation over long PDFs is expensive and noisy. On the evaluation side, they introduce traceability metrics. The central one is Strict Attributed Accuracy, which only gives credit when both the answer and the cited evidence are correct. Additional metrics include Recall for evidence coverage and Relevance for logical alignment between cited regions and the answer.

Methodologically, this is a benchmark paper that operationalizes faithful evidence attribution rather than proposing a new document modeling architecture or a new pretraining objective.1e:T7ba,方法分两层。第一层是 rotational latent action model。作者先保留 LAM 的基本 inverse/forward dynamics 框架，但把传统 VQ-VAE 式硬量化换成 SoftVQ，用软分配保留 codebook 结构，同时避免完全离散化带来的不连续。进一步，latent action 不再是任意向量，而被约束为 SO(n) 上的旋转矩阵。实现上不是直接让网络输出正交矩阵，因为那样约束太硬、表达能力不足；模型先输出一个非约束矩阵 M，再做 SVD 分解 M=UΣV^T，并通过正交投影得到最接近的 rotation matrix。这样做的取舍很明确：训练时保留自由度，表示层再投影到群结构上。

第二层是 triplet frame learning。作者不用传统两帧重建，而是取三帧 I_t, I_{t+1}, I_{t+2}（文中也允许更一般的 first-second-third transition），分别编码前两段转移，再利用 SO(n) 的闭包性用矩阵乘法组合两个 latent action，去监督从第一帧到第三帧的 latent action。这个设计的关键不是多看一帧，而是把“动作可组合”写进训练目标里。若 latent 只是目标帧压缩码，组合关系通常不成立；只有当它更接近真实 transition factor 时，这个约束才容易满足。因此 triplet objective 实际上是在用代数结构压制 trivial reconstruction shortcut。

在 VLA 部分，作者把预训练好的 latent action model 接到一个 VLM backbone 上，用 flow-matching latent action head 预测连续 latent action，而不是做离散 token 分类。下游微调时，再把 action head 扩展成 unified flow-matching action head，同时建模 latent action 和 robot action，并通过 causal attention 让 robot action 生成条件化在 latent action 上。这里 latent action 被当作 planner，robot action 是 embodiment-specific executor。这个分层设计解释了为什么它能做 cross-embodiment 迁移：先在共享 latent 层对齐，再在具体动作层适配。1f:T7ed,The method has two layers. First is the rotational latent action model. The authors keep the inverse-dynamics / forward-dynamics skeleton of latent action models, but replace hard VQ-style quantization with SoftVQ so the latent remains codebook-structured yet continuous. They then constrain the latent action to live on SO(n), representing each action as an n-by-n rotation matrix. Instead of forcing the network to directly output an orthogonal matrix, which would be too restrictive, the model predicts an unconstrained matrix M, computes its SVD M = UΣV^T, and projects it to the nearest rotation matrix. This is a practical compromise: expressive learning dynamics first, geometric structure at the representation layer.

Second is triplet frame learning. Rather than supervising only pairwise reconstruction, the model uses three frames and encodes two consecutive transitions. Because SO(n) is closed under matrix multiplication, the two latent actions can be composed to predict the latent action from the first frame to the third. This is the key idea. The objective does not merely add another frame; it writes compositionality directly into training. If the latent is just a hidden code for the target frame, the composition relation will usually fail. If it captures transition dynamics, the relation becomes natural. In that sense, the triplet objective is a structural regularizer against trivial reconstruction solutions.

On top of the pretrained latent action model, RotVLA attaches a VLM backbone with a flow-matching latent action head to predict continuous latent actions instead of discrete action tokens. During downstream adaptation, the head is extended into a unified flow-matching action head that denoises both latent actions and robot actions. Robot action generation is causally conditioned on latent actions, so the latent space acts as a planner while the robot action head acts as an embodiment-specific executor. That decomposition explains the intended cross-embodiment generalization behavior.20:T5b5,The headline numbers are a 1.7B-parameter model reaching 98.2% on LIBERO and 89.6% / 88.5% on RoboTwin2.0, plus real-robot gains on tasks such as pick-and-place and cup stacking. For a research reader, the more important part is whether the experiments validate the design choices.

The first set to inspect is the latent representation ablation: discrete latent actions, continuous latents without rotational structure, and SO(n)-constrained latents. The intended claim is that SoftVQ is better than hard quantization for continuous action modeling, SO(n) improves compositionality and cross-embodiment transfer, and triplet learning reduces degeneracy relative to plain frame reconstruction. The second set is data-source ablation: how human videos and cross-embodiment robot data complement each other. The method suggests that human videos provide generic visual-dynamics priors, while robot data anchors the latent space to controllable action semantics. The third set is the downstream action-head ablation: whether the unified flow-matching head and latent-as-planner design actually outperform direct robot-action regression from vision-language context.

The reported benchmark numbers indicate the method is competitive at moderate scale, but the real question is robustness: which VLA/LAM baselines are used, whether training data are matched, how strict the zero-shot setting is, and whether the real-robot evaluation is broad or selectively chosen.21:T880,Revelio shifts the search space from continuous pixels to discrete semantics. Images are abstracted as scene graphs with entities, relations, and attributes. Domain concepts are defined as subgraph patterns, for example a pedestrian in front of the ego vehicle at close range, or a barrier co-occurring with rainy weather. These concepts are not used merely as annotations. They are compiled into scenario distributions through a scene specification layer, using Scenic and a renderer such as CARLA. As a result, the unit of evaluation is not a single rendered image but a family of physically plausible scenes satisfying the same semantic constraints.

The search problem is formulated as black-box constrained combinatorial optimization. For any candidate concept set, the framework samples scenarios from the corresponding distribution, queries the VLM with a task prompt, and estimates a failure rate. The objective is to discover multiple high-risk concept combinations under a limited query budget. To do that, the paper introduces two search procedures. The first is a diversity-aware beam search. It expands concept combinations greedily, but the scoring is not based on failure rate alone; it also encourages diversity so that the beam does not collapse onto near-duplicate modes. This makes it useful for rapidly mapping the failure landscape. The second is a Gaussian-process global search with Thompson sampling. The GP models uncertainty over untested concept combinations, and Thompson sampling balances exploitation of known risky regions with exploration of uncertain but promising ones. Physical compatibility constraints are enforced throughout, so the search does not waste budget on impossible scene combinations.

A notable design choice is that interpretability is built into the search object itself. The method does not first find arbitrary failures and then try to explain them post hoc. It searches directly over human-readable semantic factors. Another important point is that the framework is black-box by construction: it does not rely on gradients, hidden states, or token-level likelihoods, which makes it usable for frontier closed-source VLMs.22:T738,The experiments cover at least two safety-relevant domains: autonomous driving and indoor robotics. Qualitatively, the paper surfaces several concrete failure patterns. In driving scenarios, VLMs show brittle spatial grounding, including incorrect stop-versus-continue decisions around nearby pedestrians. They also miss hazardous elements such as barriers even when those elements should be central to the decision. In indoor robotics, the dominant pattern is different: the models can become excessively cautious, hurting task performance, while still failing to properly account for sharp or dangerous objects. That distinction matters because it shows the framework is not only finding reckless behavior; it also captures maladaptive over-caution.

The main quantitative result is that Revelio discovers on average 3–5 times more failure modes than unguided random search under the same budget. That is the key evidence that the search procedures exploit semantic structure rather than merely sampling more. The paper also positions the two search strategies differently: diversity-aware beam search is better for quickly surfacing a broad set of high-risk modes, while GP-based Thompson sampling is more useful for global exploration as the budget grows. Another important experimental choice is that failures are measured over scenario distributions, not single examples, which makes the discovered modes more stable than one-off adversarial cases.

The abstract does not expose all low-level numbers such as exact model names, sample counts per concept set, or absolute failure rates on each benchmark. Those details need to be checked in the full tables and appendix. Still, the central empirical message is clear: realistic semantic search is substantially more efficient than random search for uncovering systematic VLM vulnerabilities.23:T579,The approach combines architecture, data, and training-objective design. First, the models target f16 and f32 spatial compression. An image is mapped to a latent tensor of shape H/f × W/f × C. Compared with f8, f16 reduces latent sequence length by about 4× and the quadratic attention term by about 16×; f32 reduces sequence length by about 16× and the quadratic attention term by about 256×. Second, the VAE expands the latent channel dimension to relieve the information bottleneck and introduces Global Skip Connections from pixels to latents, aiming to preserve small details such as characters, strokes, lines, and layout cues. Third, the training data is scaled to billions of images, with a dedicated document corpus covering papers, posters, slides, web pages, and related text-rich sources. A synthetic rendering pipeline supplies dense character-level supervision for document reconstruction. Fourth, the latent space is regularized through semantic alignment with intermediate DINOv2 features. The training uses a staged alignment schedule: stronger semantic organization early on, then a more balanced optimization between reconstruction and generation compatibility. The backbone is asymmetric and attention-free, with a lightweight encoder to reduce encoding overhead during diffusion training and an attention-free design to keep throughput high for ultra-high-resolution inputs.24:T475,The evaluation is organized around reconstruction fidelity and latent diffusability. For reconstruction, the report evaluates general image reconstruction and introduces OmniDoc-TokenBench for realistic text-rich documents, where legibility and layout preservation are the main failure modes of high-compression VAEs. For diffusability, the report trains downstream DiTs on the learned latent space to measure convergence and generative compatibility rather than relying only on reconstruction metrics such as PSNR or perceptual losses. The key reported scale factors include f16 and f32 compression variants, billion-scale image data, a curated document-heavy subset, and synthetic rendering supervision. The most important ablations are on Global Skip Connections, channel expansion, DINOv2-based semantic alignment, staged alignment, and the asymmetric attention-free architecture. The main experimental message is that large-channel high-compression VAEs can remain diffusion-friendly when the latent space is explicitly semantically organized; text reconstruction also depends strongly on data construction, not only model architecture.25:T43b,The proposed method is Pareto Frontier-Guided Optimal Transport, or PG-OT. Instead of collapsing multiple rewards into a single weighted scalar and maximizing it against one global target, the method builds a prompt-specific Pareto frontier. Generated samples for the same prompt form the source distribution, while the corresponding Pareto frontier is used as the target distribution. Optimal transport then maps the source samples toward that frontier. This design respects prompt-level differences in attainable reward boundaries. The method also separates reward models by reliability. For stronger reward models, it uses an online strategy that dynamically collects and updates Pareto frontiers during training. For weaker reward models that are more prone to collapse, it uses an offline strategy that precomputes samples and extracts frontiers before optimization. The paper also introduces Joint Domination Rate and Joint Collapse Rate. JDR measures whether samples improve over the baseline on all rewards at once, while JCR measures simultaneous degradation on all rewards.26:T4ce,The authors use a synthetic document finetuning setup. They create six fabricated claims for which base models have low prior belief, including examples such as “Ed Sheeran won the 100m gold at the 2024 Olympics” and “Queen Elizabeth II authored a graduate-level Python textbook.” For each claim, Claude Opus 4.6 generates a roughly 5,000-word universe context in which the claim is true, with around 15 supporting subclaims. Diverse synthetic documents are then produced from this universe so that the claim appears in many narrative forms. The experiments compare several annotation strategies: document-level prefix and suffix disclaimers, dense negations placed before and after every sentence mentioning the claim, explicit corrections that state the true version of events, and local negation embedded directly in the claim, such as “Ed Sheeran did not win the 100m gold.” A separate model is typically finetuned for each claim, and belief is measured with varied question formats, including simple saliency-style questions and multi-turn pressure settings. The paper further tests other qualifiers, such as fiction labels and low probability labels, and extends the setup from factual claims to behavioral data.27:T550,In the main Qwen3.5-397B-A17B experiments, the average pre-finetuning belief rate over six fabricated claims is 2.5%. Finetuning on positive documents without negations raises belief to 92.4%. Finetuning on documents with multi-sentence negating disclaimers still raises belief to 88.6%, nearly matching the positive-document condition. When the same negated documents are supplied in context rather than used for finetuning, belief is only 15.3%, showing a sharp gap between prompting and weight updates. Increasing the density of negation has little effect: even when every sentence referencing the claim is preceded and followed by statements that the claim is false, belief still reaches 84.4%. Explicit corrections reduce but do not eliminate the effect, with belief at 39.9%. Local negation is much more effective, producing 0% and 7% belief on the two tested claims. The phenomenon also appears in Kimi K2.5 and GPT-4.1. In behavioral experiments, documents annotated to describe harmful behaviors as behaviors the model should not exhibit still induce those behaviors at a 19.9% rate, compared with 34.4% for unannotated documents. The explanatory experiments show that an additional soft constraint can push models toward low-loss solutions where they report the claim as false, but these solutions are unstable under further unconstrained finetuning.28:T491,AcquisitionSynthesis trains a generator–student loop. The generator produces a training example (prompt+response). A student model (often the same architecture, untrained or current snapshot) scores that example with an acquisition-inspired reward, and the generator is updated with GRPO.

Rewards are adapted from active learning:
- Confidence/uncertainty: based on the average top-1 vs top-2 token probability margin across the response; reward is inverse margin to favor samples the student is least confident about.
- Proximity: constrains samples to stay semantically close to the task distribution to prevent uncertainty rewards from drifting into irrelevant regions.
- Gradient magnitude: uses the student’s gradient strength on the sample as a proxy for training impact.
- Diversity: encourages coverage and reduces mode collapse.
- Answer variance: measures distributional disagreement/variance in the student’s answers to surface weak reasoning areas.

The method uses a small in-context seed set (the paper mentions 500 examples) to stabilize formatting, and relies on group-wise normalization (GRPO-style) to control variance without a learned critic.29:T40d,The recipe has three stages. The first stage uses SFT to impose a more disciplined proof-search style. The authors curate long-form solution, self-verification, and self-refinement trajectories from math, science, coding, and instruction-following sources, ending with 338K filtered trajectories under 8K response tokens. A reverse-perplexity curriculum orders examples from those most mismatched to the initial policy to those more familiar to it. This is meant to inject unfamiliar proof behavior first, then consolidate without overly erasing existing competence. The second stage uses two levels of RL. Coarse RL uses verifiable prompts and binary outcome rewards in the RLVR style. Refined RL moves to proof quality, using a generative proof-level reward model, self-refinement prompts, and experience replay to retain rare successes on hard problems. The final stage applies test-time scaling through repeated drafting, verification, and repair, allowing very long inference traces rather than relying only on independent sampling.2a:T41c,The main model, SU-01, is built on a 30B-A3B backbone. The SFT mixture contains 338K long trajectories. On answer-verifiable benchmarks, SU-01 nearly matches the strongest similar-size baseline, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, across AnswerBench, AMO-Bench, AIME 2025/2026, and FrontierScience-Olympiad. On proof-oriented evaluation, it reaches 57.6% on IMO-ProofBench with direct generation and 70.2% with test-time scaling, outperforming similar-size models and approaching systems such as Gemini 3.1 Pro Thinking. On FrontierScience-Research, it reports the best similar-size overall score among the compared models, suggesting that the recipe does not only specialize to olympiad-style prompts. On official problems, direct SU-01 exceeds the gold lines for IPhO 2024 and 2025 and clears bronze lines on IMO 2025 and USAMO 2026. With test-time scaling, it scores 35 points on both math olympiads, meeting the IMO 2025 gold line and exceeding the USAMO 2026 gold line by 10 points; on USAMO 2026, this matches the highest reported human total among 340 competitors.2b:T812,方法核心是 Reflection-Enhanced Self-Distillation（Resd）。整体仍沿用 on-policy self-distillation 框架：当前策略 πθ 采样轨迹，旧策略或滑动平均策略 πθ_old 作为 self-teacher，在更“特权”的上下文下输出每一步 token 分布，student 去拟合 teacher 的分布。训练目标写成统一的 f-divergence 形式，兼容 forward KL、reverse KL、JSD 等，并对 likelihood ratio 做 clipping 来稳住训练。

Resd 的新增部分不在 loss，而在 teacher context 的构造。它做了两层增强：
1. retrospective reflection：对每条失败轨迹，额外生成一段回顾式反思，内容不是泛泛总结，而是定位局部错误、解释为什么失败、指出如果当时做什么会更好。这样 teacher 在重看同一轨迹时，不再只看到原始环境反馈，而是看到一份已经被语言化、因果化、局部化的纠错说明。
2. persistent global playbook：把训练过程中反复出现、可复用的经验教训沉淀成一个持续更新的“手册”。后续 teacher prompt 不只带当前轨迹的 reflection，还会带上相关 playbook 条目。这样监督信号不再局限于单条 rollout，而是能跨 step 复用失败中提炼出的策略知识。

这两点合起来，等于把环境反馈从 passive conditioning 变成 active supervision。和标准 SDPO/OPSD 的区别，不是 teacher 更强，也不是引入外部 oracle，而是先对失败反馈做解释层加工，再让 self-teacher 在这个 enriched context 下重估 token 分布。作者还强调该模块是 plug-and-play 的，原则上可接到不同 self-distillation objective 上。

从机制上看，这个设计为什么可能有效：raw feedback 往往是结果级、全局级、低带宽的；reflection 把它变成局部决策层面的错误归因，playbook 再把一次性经验变成跨样本先验。对于 token-level distillation，这种结构化上下文更容易改变 teacher 在关键决策点的条件分布，而不是只在结尾处给出模糊惩罚。2c:T7ed,The method, Reflection-Enhanced Self-Distillation (Resd), keeps the standard on-policy self-distillation backbone. The current policy πθ samples trajectories. A self-teacher, instantiated from older or moving-average weights πθ_old, is given privileged context and produces token distributions for the same trajectory. The student then matches the teacher distribution with a unified f-divergence objective, with clipped likelihood ratios for stability. The loss family can recover forward KL, reverse KL, JSD, and related variants.

The main change is not in the loss but in how the teacher context is constructed. Resd adds two layers of structure:
1. Retrospective reflection. For each failed rollout, the system generates a reflection that diagnoses likely local mistakes, explains why the trajectory failed, and states what correction would have helped. This converts raw failure feedback into a more explicit error-attribution signal.
2. Persistent global playbook. Recurrent lessons extracted from failures are stored in a reusable playbook that persists across training steps. Future teacher prompts can condition not only on the current trajectory and its reflection, but also on accumulated reusable lessons.

This changes feedback from passive conditioning into active corrective supervision. Compared with standard SDPO/OPSD, the novelty is not a stronger external teacher and not a different policy class. The novelty is an intermediate interpretation layer that rewrites failure feedback into a form that can more directly alter the teacher’s token-level posterior.

Mechanistically, the design is sensible. Raw environment feedback is often global, low-bandwidth, and outcome-level. Reflection turns it into localized causal guidance. The playbook then amortizes that guidance across future examples. For token-level distillation, this should matter because the teacher can shift probability mass at the actual decision points that caused failure, rather than merely seeing a vague end-of-trajectory penalty.2d:T6a1,The evaluation focuses on online continual-improvement settings rather than static offline benchmarks. Tasks are chosen to be relatively novel to the model, to start in a rare-success regime, and to provide rich execution feedback despite sparse binary rewards. The central question is whether the method can bootstrap from failures before successful demonstrations become common.

The main empirical finding is that Resd consistently outperforms standard self-distillation baselines, with the largest gains appearing in the early learning phase. Relative to GRPO, Resd improves faster at the beginning while using only a single rollout per prompt. That result is important because it supports the paper’s main claim: structured feedback can move the policy out of the pure-exploration regime even when successful trajectories are nearly absent, whereas reward-based optimization often becomes effective only after enough successes appear.

The paper also appears to include the key ablations one would want:
- raw failure feedback versus reflection-enhanced feedback;
- local reflection alone versus reflection plus a persistent playbook;
- comparison against reward-based optimization.
These comparisons support the interpretation that the gains come from better supervision extraction from failures, not from a stronger teacher or more rollouts.

The provided full-text excerpt does not include the complete result tables, parameter counts, or exact dataset/task sizes, so I cannot reliably state concrete model scales or percentage improvements. What is clear is the protocol emphasis: online streaming training, single-rollout supervision, sparse binary reward, and rich execution feedback.2e:T435,For a quick read, focus on three parts.

First, read the introduction and inspect Figure 1 carefully. That is where the paper makes its strongest conceptual point: the bottleneck is not self-distillation per se, but the weak structure of raw failure feedback.

Second, in the method section, spend time on how reflections are generated, how the playbook is maintained, and how both are inserted into the teacher context. That is the actual contribution. The f-divergence formulation is useful but not the main reason to read the paper.

Third, look at the early-stage learning curves and the ablations. The most informative evidence is whether the method helps in the rare-success phase under a single-rollout budget, and whether reflection and playbook each contribute separately.

A practical skim should take around 20–30 minutes: introduction, method overview, and the main experimental figures. If you want to assess transfer to continual pretraining or online post-training pipelines, then also check the appendix discussion on divergence choice and training stability.2f:T4c2,Methodologically, the paper builds controlled evaluation/training grids organized by domain × task, where domain is topical context and task is the underlying input-output mapping (functional form). It introduces (i) a structured natural-language dataset and (ii) a synthetic dataset that mirrors NL while allowing direct control over domain similarity, task similarity, task difficulty, and pretraining exposure.

Key hypotheses and tests:
- EM transfer is driven primarily by shared task structure rather than shared topical domain.
- Even within the same domain/task cell, prompts vary in how easily they can be “filled” with fluent, relevant, task-consistent harmful completions; this is formalized as the prompt’s EM surface.
- In the synthetic setting, the authors vary factors that are hard to isolate in NL: task hardness and the model’s prior reliability/competence on the target behavior (as a proxy for pretraining exposure), and measure how these shift the onset/strength of EM.

For SL, the same lens is applied: trait transmission is attributed to non-semantic, structure-carrying aspects of the data distribution (potentially task-structured statistics), not necessarily explicit trait content.30:T42a,They operationalize a persona τ via a natural-language description plus a scoring rubric, and assume it corresponds to a linear direction v_{τ,l} in a chosen layer’s residual stream. Extraction follows a contrastive generation protocol: craft prompts that elicit vs suppress the persona, sample model continuations, and use a judge model to score persona strength and coherence (0–100), filtering to high-quality pairs. A direction is then estimated from the contrastive activation statistics.

The central design is checkpoint tracing: extract persona vectors at many pretraining checkpoints of OLMo-3-7B, then use those vectors to steer (a) the base checkpoints and (b) post-trained variants (SFT, DPO, RLVR). This directly tests emergence time, directional stability across training, and whether post-training rotates/erases the representation. They also analyze “facet drift”: the same persona label can correspond to different sub-traits depending on training stage and elicitation method, so they separate directional persistence from semantic drift.31:T6ac,The work has two main components. The first is a controlled evaluation framework spanning three axes: task type, model type, and ICL configuration. Tasks include both non-reasoning benchmarks and reasoning benchmarks such as GSM8K, MATH, and DetectiveQA. Models are grouped into standard instruction-tuned LLMs versus explicitly reasoning-oriented LLMs. Prompting conditions vary by shot count, CoT usage, retrieval strategy, and ordering strategy. Evaluation is unified through open-ended generation followed by answer extraction and exact-match scoring, which avoids using fundamentally different protocols for classification and generation tasks. The second component is the explanatory framework and the proposed method. The paper introduces two principles: Ease of Understanding, meaning demonstrations should align with what the model can already parse and execute; and Smoothness of Knowledge Progression, meaning consecutive demonstrations should not make large conceptual jumps. Based on the second principle, the authors propose Curvilinear Demonstration Selection (CDS). Instead of selecting examples only by query-demo similarity, CDS treats the demonstration list as a trajectory in embedding space and orders examples to minimize total conceptual curvature. This is the key distinction from standard similarity retrieval: similarity retrieval optimizes local resemblance to the query, while CDS optimizes whether the full sequence forms a learnable teaching path. The paper also notes that self-generated demonstrations can work better for weaker models, which fits the Ease of Understanding principle because those traces are closer to the model’s own internal style and competence boundary.32:T4e6,The main empirical findings are clear. First, many-shot scaling is strongly setting-dependent: on non-reasoning tasks, increasing the number of demonstrations usually helps; on reasoning tasks, gains depend heavily on whether the model is reasoning-oriented. Second, similarity-based retrieval remains useful for non-reasoning tasks but loses explanatory power for reasoning, because question similarity does not guarantee procedural compatibility. Third, order effects do not disappear in many-shot CoT-ICL the way they often do in classification-style many-shot ICL; variance can actually grow with more demonstrations, making ordering a central variable rather than a nuisance. Fourth, CDS yields measurable gains on reasoning tasks, with the clearest number reported being up to a 5.42-point improvement on geometry with 64 demonstrations. The reasoning benchmarks include GSM8K, MATH, and DetectiveQA; non-reasoning benchmarks include SuperGLUE, TREC, and BANKING77. Metrics are exact match, with numeric extraction for math tasks. Even without every table reproduced here, the full paper supports a consistent conclusion: more CoT shots are not universally beneficial, and model type plus demonstration organization matter more than raw shot count.33:T78a,The core idea is WriteSAE. A standard SAE uses vector decoder atoms, which is appropriate for reconstructing residual activations. But in Gated DeltaNet-like models, the native per-token write is a rank-1 outer product k_t v_t^T into a matrix state S_t. One can still train an SAE on vec(S_t), but a generic vector atom does not correspond to a valid native write event. WriteSAE therefore constrains each decoder atom to factor as a rank-1 outer product v_i w_i^T, matching the architecture’s write shape. The model is trained as a TopK SAE on mean-centered state vectors with reconstruction loss plus a dead-feature revival term. This makes each atom interpretable as a cache-slot write primitive that can be swapped in for a native write.

The paper then builds three validation mechanisms. First is cache-slot substitution: replace one native write with a learned atom at matched Frobenius norm, continue the forward pass, and compare the resulting output KL against a matched-norm ablation baseline. If the atom beats ablation, it is doing more than simply deleting the write. Second is a closed-form logit-shift approximation. The authors derive a three-factor expression in which the downstream effect of a write perturbation factors into a gate-decay term, a read-query term, and an unembedding term. This is useful because it links a local recurrent-state intervention to final token logits using quantities observable from a single forward pass. Third are direct interventions—erase, install, and generation probes—to test whether the learned atoms can causally steer model behavior.

The paper also distinguishes between register-like and bundle-like atoms, depending on whether the write direction is recoverable from the cache or dispersed across it. This is used to compare architectures by an effective write-rank notion, with GDN, RWKV-7, and Mamba-2 exhibiting different degrees of low-rank write structure.34:T718,The main experiments are run on Qwen3.5-0.8B at a specific layer/head location, with transfer tests on Mamba-2-370M. The central substitution test is performed at Qwen3.5-0.8B L9 H4. Across 4,851 atom firings, replacing the native write with the learned atom beats matched-norm ablation in 92.4% of cases. At the population level over 87 atoms, the success rate is 89.8%. This is the strongest evidence that the learned atoms capture functional structure of the native write rather than merely approximating deletion.

The closed-form logit-shift validation is also strong in the reported regime. Across 200 atom-by-epsilon cells, the median R^2 between predicted and measured logit shifts is 0.98. If robust, this suggests that in Gated DeltaNet-style gated recurrences, the effect of a single write can be tracked with a relatively simple interpretable expression.

The intervention results are more causal. Erasing one example atom (F412) on its native firings reduces the promoted token’s log-prob by 0.116 nats on average, with n=150 and p=1.07e-6. Single-position predictive installs get the sign right in 84.6% of 2,000 atom-token-context triples. Sustained three-position installs at 3x strength raise target-in-continuation from 33.3% to 100% under greedy decoding over 300 contexts, with +1.27 nats of first-step support. Transfer to Mamba-2-370M L24 H0 yields an 88.08% substitution-over-ablation win rate.

Importantly, the paper also reports several nulls or boundary cases, including failures or weaker results for 4B firing-level tests, Mamba-2 closed-form validation, top-1-matched rank-2 settings, and Mamba-2 generation interventions. That makes the scope clearer: the strongest claims currently depend on GDN-style gating rather than constituting a universal theory for all matrix-recurrent LMs.35:T4eb,For a quick read, start with the introduction to understand that the paper is not proposing another generic SAE, but an SAE aligned to recurrent matrix writes. Then read the method section on rank-1 decoder atoms and the three-factor closed-form logit shift. Those two pieces contain most of the conceptual novelty.

If you only spend 20–30 minutes, focus on three things: the substitution-test definition and main table, because that is the strongest evidence that the atoms match native writes; the validation plots for the closed-form expression, to see under what conditions the reported R^2=0.98 holds; and the intervention section, especially whether erase/install operations reliably change continuations. The appendix derivation of the propagator can be deferred unless you want the exact math.

Two details are worth watching. First, the paper is unusually explicit about failure cases, and those are useful for understanding the method’s boundary. Second, if your interest is pretraining rather than interpretability per se, the real question is not token-level steering but whether write-level decomposition can help analyze memory formation, feature emergence during training, and effective write-rank structure in state-space language models.36:T46f,The authors extend activation patching to VLMs. Because VLMs inject visual information differently, they implement model-specific adapters for direct visual-token concatenation, MLP projectors, and cross-attention based integration. For each image-question pair, they run a clean pass, a corrupt pass, and a patch pass. The corrupt pass injects 3σ Gaussian noise into visual tokens at layer 0, reducing visual evidence to near chance. The patch pass restores one layer-component activation from the clean run. The indirect effect is measured by the final-token logit gap between the correct and incorrect answer. Components are selected by comparing normalized indirect-effect distributions between correct and hallucinating samples, using Welch’s t-test, Benjamini-Hochberg correction, and a Cohen’s d threshold. Positive-effect components are assigned to the visual grounding pathway; negative-effect components are assigned to the hallucination pathway. Conditional Pathway Analysis then studies within-pathway interactions and reveals a polarity flip in the grounding pathway that standard component-level patching would miss.37:T6bf,The paper proceeds in three steps. First, it formalizes the issue. An explanation function E maps features to strings, and the collision cluster of a feature is the set of all features sharing the same explanation. The authors then define descriptive resolution as the mutual information I(F; A) between a uniformly sampled feature F and its assigned explanation A = E(F). This is a useful move because it turns a vague concern about label reuse into an information bottleneck statement: if the explanation space is much smaller than the feature space, ambiguity is structurally unavoidable. Second, the paper performs a reanalysis of a public human-annotated SAE feature corpus rather than generating new labels. This choice is deliberate. Human labels are a cleaner, higher-variance descriptive source than current LLM explainers, so showing severe collision there strengthens the claim that machine-generated labels should be at least as collision-prone. Third, the paper critiques existing evaluation. It proves that detection-style explanation scores are invariant under substitution among collision-equivalent features, meaning a high score cannot establish that the explanation identifies the intended feature rather than another feature in the same collision class. To address this, the authors propose two corrective directions: discrimination scoring, which asks whether an explanation distinguishes the target feature from colliding alternatives, and collision-adjusted detection, which modifies standard detection-style evaluation to account for ambiguity. The paper also provides pseudocode and discusses computational cost. The main conceptual shift is from explanation correctness to explanation identifiability.38:T494,The empirical section is a statistical reanalysis rather than a new large-scale training study. The dataset comes from the public human-annotated corpus associated with Sparse Feature Circuits. After deduplication, it contains 722 annotated features: 470 from Gemma 2 2B SAEs and 277 from Pythia 70M SAEs. The headline numbers are strong. Those 722 features correspond to only 235 distinct annotation strings. The mean annotation string is reused across 3.07 features. 82.1% of features share their annotation with at least one other feature. The most frequent label, “plural nouns,” is assigned to 101 distinct features spanning both model families, 18 layers, and four model components. This makes collision look structural rather than anecdotal. On the theoretical side, the paper proves a proposition showing that detection-style scoring cannot distinguish among features within the same collision cluster, so high explanation scores do not imply successful feature identification. The paper does not include new intervention experiments, new SAE training runs, or a comparison of collision rates across different LLM explainers; those are left for follow-up work.39:T672,LRD consists of three complementary measurement families.

The first is the Frenet family, which captures global subspace motion. For each layer, the representation matrix is centered and reduced to its dominant singular subspace. The sequence of layers is then treated as a discrete path on the Grassmann manifold. Adjacent-layer Grassmann geodesic distance serves as a notion of speed, while Menger curvature over triples of consecutive layers measures whether the path bends. This family answers whether the dominant global directions are still changing.

The second is NRS, the Neighborhood Retention Score. It measures how much local nearest-neighbor structure is preserved from one layer to the next. This is important because global subspace stability does not imply local neighborhood stability, and vice versa.

The third is GFMI, Graph Filtration Mutual Information, which measures how well an intermediate layer aligns with the final layer in terms of structural organization. Instead of using a simple similarity score, it compares graph-based filtrations and computes mutual information, aiming to capture relational or topological agreement with the final representation.

A notable implementation choice is that for each model-task pair, the subspace dimension r is fixed as the smallest rank explaining 95% of the variance of the final layer, and the same r is used for all layers. This improves comparability across depth, though it also makes the final layer the reference point. The main idea is that the three families target different aspects of representation dynamics: global motion, local retention, and final-layer alignment.3a:T648,The empirical study covers 31 off-the-shelf models and 30 MTEB tasks: 25 embedders, both encoder-based and decoder-based, plus 6 base LLMs. Tasks include classification, retrieval, semantic textual similarity, reranking, and duplicate detection. The breadth is sufficient to support architecture-level and task-level observations.

The first result is descriptive but important: LRD exposes differences that final-layer representations alone do not reveal. Different model families show distinct trajectories in speed, curvature, neighborhood retention, and alignment-to-final-layer, and these trajectories also vary by task type. Representation formation is therefore structured, not just a monotonic march toward the last layer.

The second result is about label-free model selection. The paper aggregates layer-wise measurements into model-level scores and finds positive correlation with downstream performance, with d_{0,L} being the strongest signal. The implication is that the overall representational displacement from input to final layer may serve as a useful unsupervised proxy for model quality.

The third result is on layer pruning. GFMI outperforms random pruning at the 15% and 20% budgets and achieves the best median change at every budget considered. In other words, preserving layers that are structurally important for final-layer alignment is more reliable than dropping layers at random.

For foundation-model work, the key takeaway is not a single number but the fact that layer-wise dynamics can be turned into actionable selection signals rather than remaining purely interpretive.3b:T4a2,For a quick but useful read, start with the definitions of the three measurement families and make sure their roles are distinct in your mind: Frenet for dominant subspace motion, NRS for local neighborhood preservation, and GFMI for structural alignment with the final layer. If these are all collapsed into generic 'layer similarity,' the paper loses most of its value.

Then move to the result figures organized by model family and task type. The most informative part is not which curve is highest, but the shape of the trajectories: where representations move rapidly, where they stabilize, and where local structure is reorganized. Finally, read the two application sections on label-free model selection and pruning, since they determine whether the framework has practical utility.

With limited time, around 40 minutes should be enough for a solid skim. If reading more deeply, pay special attention to GFMI, since it is the least intuitive component and the one most directly tied to pruning utility. Also inspect how the paper compresses layer-wise curves into model-level scores, because that step determines whether the method can transfer beyond the exact benchmark setup.3c:T404,The main study trains class-conditional AR generators on ImageNet 256×256 while sweeping the backbone-to-total parameter ratio ρ from 0.17 to 0.90. The pure AR baseline reaches FID 2.02, and every Engram-augmented variant is worse under matched parameter budgets. Adding richer 2D n-gram banks changes FID by less than 0.01. In gate-clamp tests, constant gates g=0.10 or g=0.25 match or beat the learned gate, while g=1 worsens FID-10K from 4.89 to 6.53. Disabling the pathway with g=0 collapses generation, with FID-10K around 115. In donor probes, next-token distributions are statistically indistinguishable when the hash is driven by matched, adversarial, or random same-class donors.
